{/* === ERA 3: FRENCH REVOLUTION === */}

## 3. French Revolution (1789 to 1799)

> "Liberte, Egalite, Fraternite." Era 3 of 12. Bread, the Bastille, the guillotine, and a republic eating its own.

On the morning of July 14, 1789, **Bernard-Rene Jordan de Launay**, governor of the Bastille, looks out from his window over a courtyard of about 900 Parisians demanding gunpowder. They are not, on this morning, an organized political force. They are bakers and apprentices and porters and out-of-work veterans, and they are short on bread and short on patience. They want the gunpowder stockpiled inside the fortress because they have heard a rumor that **Louis XVI** is planning to use the army to dissolve the National Assembly. By mid-afternoon Launay has refused, then surrendered, then been killed; his head is paraded on a pike to the Place de Greve. The Bastille had held seven prisoners that morning. None of them mattered. What mattered was the symbol: the king's fortress had fallen to a Paris mob, and the mob had won. France would never go back.

<Callout type="insight" title="Why this matters">
  The French Revolution is the moment Enlightenment ideas leave the salon and hit the street. It is also the moment those ideas turn on themselves. By 1794, a movement that began with the slogan "Liberty, Equality, Fraternity" is executing about 17,000 people, most of them peasants and middle-class. The Revolution is the test case for every later debate about whether radical change is worth its costs.
</Callout>

### Timeline

  { date: 'July 27, 1794', title: 'Thermidorian Reaction', body: '9 Thermidor, Year II. The Convention turns on Robespierre. He is arrested and guillotined the next day. The Terror ends.', icon: 'X' },

### Cause

By the late 1780s France looked, on the surface, like Europe's premier power. About 28 million people, the largest army on the continent, the most sophisticated culture. Underneath, three crises ran in parallel and converged.

The **social** crisis was the structure of the **<KeyTerm term="Old Regime">The pre-1789 social and political order in France: divine-right monarchy plus three estates plus feudal privileges. Swept away in months by the Revolution.</KeyTerm>**. France was legally divided into three estates. The **First Estate** was the Catholic clergy: about 130,000 people, owning 10% of French land, paying almost no taxes. The **Second Estate** was the nobility: about 400,000 people (around 2% of the population), owning 20% of the land, also exempt from most taxes. The **Third Estate** was everyone else: 97% of the population. Inside the Third Estate, the **bourgeoisie** (the middle class of merchants, lawyers, and professionals) were educated and inspired by the Enlightenment but excluded from political power. The **urban workers** were poorly paid and often unemployed. The **peasants**, about 80% of the population, paid roughly half their income in taxes (combining royal taxes, Church tithes, and feudal dues to local nobles).

  title="The three estates in 1789: population versus land ownership versus tax burden"

/>

The **economic** crisis was real. Two decades of expensive wars (the Seven Years' War of 1756 to 1763 had cost France its empire in North America and India; American independence in 1776 to 1783 cost France another 1.3 billion livres in support of the rebels) had pushed the royal treasury to bankruptcy. **Marie Antoinette**, the Austrian-born queen, became known as **"Madame Deficit"** for her well-publicized spending on dresses, jewelry, and the small theatrical hamlet at Versailles where she pretended to be a milkmaid. By 1788 the government was paying about half its budget on debt service alone. Banks refused to lend more.

Bread made the abstraction concrete. A typical Parisian working family ate three to four loaves of bread per day. Bread was the cheapest source of calories and the central staple of the diet. By July 1789 a bad harvest in 1788 plus government finance pressure had pushed the price of a four-pound loaf to about 14 sous, when a working-class male wage was about 25 sous a day. Bread alone was eating about half the household budget. Hungry people are the rawest political material there is.

<Callout type="definition" title="The bread math, 1789">
  Family of four, three to four loaves per day, half of working wages going to bread alone, harvest failure in 1788 lifting the price of grain. Empty bellies translate into politics in a way no Enlightenment pamphlet ever could.
</Callout>

The **political** crisis was the king himself. **Louis XVI** was indecisive, distracted, and inclined to delegate. His finance ministers (Turgot, Necker, Calonne) all proposed reforms that would have taxed the nobility. The nobility refused. Louis backed down. In May 1789, with bankruptcy imminent, he was forced to convene the **<KeyTerm term="Estates-General">A medieval French assembly of the three estates (clergy, nobility, commoners), called by Louis XVI in May 1789 for the first time in 175 years to address the financial crisis. The Third Estate broke away to form the National Assembly.</KeyTerm>** at Versailles for the first time in 175 years.

#### The map of France on the eve of revolution

    { lat: 45.7640, lng: 4.8357, label: 'Lyon', popup: 'Mass executions during the Reign of Terror, 1793 to 1794. Federalist revolt was crushed.' },

    { lat: 43.6047, lng: 1.4442, label: 'Toulouse', popup: 'Center of southern resistance to the Revolution. Mixed Catholic and federalist sympathies.' },
    { lat: 50.6292, lng: 3.0573, label: 'Lille', popup: 'Northern France. Garrison town near the Austrian Netherlands. Front line in the 1792 to 1794 wars.' }
  ]}
/>

The geography mattered. Paris, with about 600,000 people, dominated French politics by sheer weight of population and proximity to Versailles. The peasant Vendee in the west and the federalist Lyon in the southeast became the two great theaters of internal counter-revolution. The eastern border, where Louis XVI tried and failed to flee at Varennes, was the corridor through which Austrian and Prussian armies would invade in 1792.

The Estates-General met for six weeks in deadlock. Each estate had one collective vote, so the First and Second Estates could always outvote the Third even though the Third represented 97% of the population. On June 17, 1789, the Third Estate broke away, declared itself the **<KeyTerm term="National Assembly">Self-declared by the Third Estate in June 1789. Took the Tennis Court Oath promising not to disband until France had a constitution. Became the legislative body of the early Revolution.</KeyTerm>**, and claimed to speak for the nation. Three days later, locked out of their usual meeting hall, they gathered at a nearby indoor tennis court and swore the **<KeyTerm term="Tennis Court Oath">June 20, 1789. The Third Estate, locked out of their meeting hall, gathered at a tennis court and swore not to disband until France had a constitution. The first formal break from the Old Regime.</KeyTerm>**: they would not disband until France had a constitution.

Three weeks later, on July 14, the **<KeyTerm term="Bastille">A royal fortress and political prison in Paris. Stormed July 14, 1789, by a Paris crowd looking for gunpowder. Symbolically marked the end of the Old Regime; July 14 is now France's national holiday.</KeyTerm>** fell.

The fall of the Bastille was the political crystallization. Within three weeks, the Great Fear (a wave of rural panic and peasant attacks on noble estates) forced the Assembly to abolish feudal privileges in a single all-night session on August 4, 1789. Three weeks after that, on August 26, the Assembly issued the **<KeyTerm term="Declaration of the Rights of Man">August 26, 1789. France's Enlightenment manifesto: men are born free and equal in rights; sovereignty resides in the nation; law is the expression of the general will. Permanent foundation of European human-rights thinking.</KeyTerm>**.

<DocAnnotate
  title="Declaration of the Rights of Man and Citizen, August 26, 1789 (excerpt)"
  text={`Article 1: Men are born and remain free and equal in rights. Social distinctions can be founded only on the common utility. Article 4: Liberty consists of doing anything which does not harm others; thus, the exercise of the natural rights of every man has only those borders which assure other members of the society the fruition of these same rights. Article 6: The law is the expression of the general will. All citizens have the right to take part personally, or by their representatives, in its formation. It must be the same for all, whether it protects or punishes.`}
  annotations={[
    { start: 0, end: 51, label: 'Free and equal', note: 'Direct echo of Locke and the American Declaration of 1776. Foundational claim of modern human rights.', color: '#fee2e2' },
    { start: 113, end: 200, label: 'Liberty defined', note: 'A negative-liberty definition: you are free to do what does not harm others. The minimal Enlightenment frame.', color: '#dbeafe' },
    { start: 309, end: 367, label: 'Rousseau\'s general will', note: 'Direct quote from Rousseau\'s Social Contract. The law is what the people, collectively, decide.', color: '#dcfce7' },
    { start: 421, end: 500, label: 'Equal application', note: 'Equal protection: the law treats everyone the same. Foundational anti-Old-Regime principle.', color: '#fef3c7' }
  ]}
/>

The Declaration was the Enlightenment compressed into seventeen articles. **Article 1** echoes Locke and Jefferson. **Article 4** defines liberty negatively (you are free to do what does not harm others). **Article 6** quotes Rousseau directly. The Declaration was, however, conspicuously silent about women. **Olympe de Gouges**, in 1791, would publish a *Declaration of the Rights of Woman and the Female Citizen* in response. She was guillotined in 1793.

In October 1789 the **Women's March on Versailles**, driven by bread shortages, brought thousands of Parisian women twelve miles to the palace, where they demanded the king and queen come back to Paris. The royal family was now confined to the Tuileries, under closer popular watch. In July 1790 the Assembly passed the **Civil Constitution of the Clergy**, seizing Church land to pay off the national debt and turning priests into salaried state employees who had to swear loyalty to the constitution. About half the French clergy refused, splitting the country between "constitutional" and "refractory" priests; this was the single biggest mistake the Revolution made in alienating rural Catholic France from its political project.

By June 1791 Louis XVI tried to flee France with his family. The royal coach was caught at **Varennes**, near the eastern border. The escape attempt destroyed any remaining trust between the king and the Revolution. By the time the Constitution of 1791 was signed, formally making France a constitutional monarchy with a Legislative Assembly, almost no one believed Louis XVI accepted it.

Inside the Legislative Assembly, factions hardened. **Radicals** sat on the left and pushed for a republic; **moderates** in the center wanted constitutional monarchy on Locke's model; **conservatives** on the right wanted to restore the Old Regime. Outside the Assembly, two new groups dominated street politics. **<KeyTerm term="Sans-Culottes">Literally those without knee-breeches. Working-class radical Parisians who wore long trousers as a class marker. Pressured the Convention from the streets and powered the Reign of Terror.</KeyTerm>** were Parisian working-class radicals, named after the long trousers they wore instead of the aristocratic knee-breeches (culottes). **Emigres** were nobles and royalist clergy who had fled France and were lobbying European courts to invade and restore the Old Regime.

In April 1792 the Legislative Assembly declared war on Austria and Prussia. Other European monarchies joined. The Revolution was now fighting all of conservative Europe. By that summer Paris was in panic. In the **September Massacres** of 1792, mobs killed about 1,300 royal guards and prisoners, fearing they would form a fifth column when the Prussians reached Paris. The Legislative Assembly, discredited, abolished the monarchy and called elections for a new body, the **National Convention**.

The Convention, dominated by the radical **<KeyTerm term="Jacobins">Radical revolutionary faction led by Robespierre. Argued for a centralized republic and revolutionary virtue. Drove the Reign of Terror through the Committee of Public Safety.</KeyTerm>**, declared France a republic on September 22, 1792. All adult males could vote and hold office (women still could not). Louis XVI was tried for treason, convicted by a margin of one vote on the death penalty, and **guillotined on January 21, 1793** in the Place de la Revolution. He died well, by all accounts. His last words were drowned out by drums.

By summer 1793 the Convention faced civil war in the Vendee, foreign invasion on multiple fronts, and economic collapse. **Maximilien Robespierre**, an austere lawyer from Arras, rose to dominate the **Committee of Public Safety**, the twelve-man committee that effectively ran France from the spring of 1793. Robespierre's stated goal was a **"Republic of Virtue"**, a regenerated society in which Old Regime habits had been burned out and replaced by Enlightenment ideals. The instrument was terror.

#### The phases of the Revolution

| Phase | Dates | Government | Defining moment |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Moderate** | 1789 to 1791 | National / Constituent Assembly, then Legislative Assembly | Storming of the Bastille; Declaration of the Rights of Man; Constitutional Monarchy |
| **Radical** | 1792 to 1794 | National Convention; Committee of Public Safety | Execution of Louis XVI; Reign of Terror; Robespierre |
| **Reaction** | 1795 to 1799 | The Directory | Thermidorian Reaction; Napoleon's coup of 18 Brumaire |

The **<KeyTerm term="Reign of Terror">June 1793 to July 1794. Robespierre and the Committee of Public Safety executed political opponents at industrial scale via the guillotine. About 17,000 official executions; thousands more died in prison.</KeyTerm>** ran from June 1793 to July 1794. About 17,000 official executions by **<KeyTerm term="Guillotine">Mechanical execution device perfected in 1789. Sold to the public as humane and democratic, equal in death for noble and commoner. Used at industrial scale during the Reign of Terror.</KeyTerm>**; thousands more died in prison or in extra-judicial massacres in the provinces. Notably, **85% of victims were peasants and middle-class**, not nobles or clergy. The popular image of the guillotine as a noble-killer is wrong on the data: most of the people on the cart were ordinary citizens accused of disloyalty by neighbors, soldiers, or revolutionary tribunals.

<GuillotineAnimation />

The guillotine was sold to the French public as a humanitarian advance. Before 1789, public executions in France had varied by class: nobles got the sword (quick if the executioner was skilled), commoners got the rope or the wheel (slow and excruciating). The guillotine, designed by **Dr. Joseph-Ignace Guillotin** and perfected by Dr. Antoine Louis, killed everyone the same way: a falling angled blade, severance in less than a second, by all medical accounts the most reliable method then known. Equality in death was the selling point. In practice, the guillotine became an industrial-scale political weapon. Trials happened in the morning, executions in the afternoon, and the cart traveled to and from the Place de la Revolution all summer of 1794.

### The Robespierre decision

By July 1794, the Terror had eaten its own. Robespierre had purged the **Hebertists** (the most radical sans-culottes faction) in March; he had purged the **Dantonists** (the more moderate Jacobins, including his old friend Georges Danton) in April. After six weeks of brooding withdrawal, he returned to the Convention on 8 Thermidor (July 26) and delivered a long speech vaguely accusing unnamed conspirators of treason. His refusal to name them terrified the deputies, who all assumed they were on the list. That night, Tallien, Fouche, Barras, Billaud-Varenne, and Collot d'Herbois conspired against him. The next morning, 9 Thermidor, the Convention turned.

You are now Robespierre. The morning of 9 Thermidor. What do you do?

<BranchingScenario
  title="You are Robespierre on 9 Thermidor"
  context="Paris, July 27, 1794 (9 Thermidor, Year II of the French Revolutionary calendar). Yesterday you delivered a long speech accusing unnamed conspirators of treason. Tonight Tallien, Fouche, Barras, and Billaud-Varenne are coordinating against you. The Convention reconvenes in three hours. Five branching paths. One historical truth."
  tree={robespierreTree}
  historicalAnswer="Robespierre tried to defend himself in the Convention on 9 Thermidor and was shouted down four times. The Convention voted for his arrest by acclamation. The Paris Commune attempted to rescue him, but the rain dispersed the sans-culotte battalions, the Commune failed to force the issue, and Barras's National Guard stormed the Hotel de Ville at about 2 AM on 10 Thermidor. Robespierre's jaw was shattered, by his own hand or a soldier's pistol. He was guillotined that afternoon, July 28, 1794, with twenty-one others. The Reign of Terror ended within days. The Thermidorian Reaction began."
/>

The historical Robespierre took the conciliatory approach, tried to defend himself in the Convention, was shouted down, and was arrested by acclamation. The Paris Commune attempted to rescue him; the rain dispersed the sans-culottes; the rescue failed. He was guillotined on July 28, 1794, the day after his arrest. The **<KeyTerm term="Thermidorian Reaction">July 27, 1794. Convention members fearful of being purged next moved against Robespierre. He was guillotined the next day. Marked the end of the Reign of Terror.</KeyTerm>** ended the Terror. Within a year the Convention had drafted a new constitution, replacing itself with the **<KeyTerm term="Directory">The five-man executive committee that governed France 1795 to 1799 after the Terror. Corrupt, weak, and overthrown by Napoleon's coup of 18 Brumaire.</KeyTerm>**: a five-man executive plus a two-house legislature.

The Directory was stable on paper and corrupt in practice. It relied increasingly on the army (and on a young Corsican general named **Napoleon Bonaparte**) to keep order in Paris and to win wars on the borders. By November 1799 the Directory was so unpopular that Napoleon's coup of 18 Brumaire dissolved it without a fight. The French Revolution, in any meaningful sense, was over. Era 4 begins.

#### Causal chain, in one diagram

```mermaid

  A[Royal bankruptcy from wars,<br/>1763 to 1789] --> B[Louis XVI calls<br/>Estates-General, May 1789]
  C[Bad harvest 1788,<br/>bread crisis] --> B
  D[Enlightenment ideas<br/>via salons and pamphlets] --> B
  E[American Revolution<br/>example, 1776 to 1783] --> B
  B --> F[Estates deadlock]

  G --> H[Tennis Court Oath,<br/>June 20, 1789]

  N --> O[Constitutional Monarchy,<br/>September 1791]
  O --> P[War with Austria + Prussia,<br/>April 1792]
  P --> Q[September Massacres,<br/>September 1792]
  Q --> R[National Convention,<br/>Republic declared,<br/>September 22, 1792]
  R --> S[Louis XVI guillotined,<br/>January 21, 1793]
  S --> T[Reign of Terror under<br/>Robespierre, 1793 to 1794]
  T --> U[Thermidorian Reaction,<br/>July 27, 1794]
  U --> V[The Directory,<br/>1795 to 1799]
  V --> W[Napoleon's coup of<br/>18 Brumaire, November 1799]
```

#### Inside the radical phase

The Convention is, on paper, the most democratic body France had ever assembled. Universal manhood suffrage. No property qualification. About 750 deputies representing every region of France. In practice, the radical Jacobins dominated the Committee of Public Safety, which over the course of 1793 became the executive arm of the Republic. The Committee included Robespierre, Saint-Just (the youngest member, 25 in 1793), Couthon, Carnot ("the Organizer of Victory" who handled military supply), Billaud-Varenne, and seven others. They met in a small room in the Tuileries and signed off on policy, denunciations, arrest warrants, and law-of-22-Prairial expedited tribunals.

Outside Paris, the Republic was at war on multiple fronts. Austrian and Prussian armies on the eastern border. Spanish forces from the Pyrenees. British naval blockade. And, crucially, a counter-revolutionary uprising inside France itself, in the **Vendee** region of western France, where peasants outraged by the Civil Constitution of the Clergy and by mass conscription had formed peasant armies. The Vendee war from 1793 to 1796 killed perhaps 200,000 people, more than the official Reign of Terror, and is sometimes called France's "first modern civil war." The Republic crushed it brutally; some historians have called the campaign in the Vendee an early modern case of mass atrocity.

The Reign of Terror was not, despite the name, only about the guillotine. Drowning sentences ("Republican baptisms") in Nantes killed thousands. Mass shootings at Lyon killed thousands more. The Law of 22 Prairial (June 1794) abolished defense counsel and limited verdicts to acquittal or death. By July 1794, even loyal Jacobins were terrified. That terror, more than any external threat, brought down Robespierre on 9 Thermidor.

The wars also produced something the Old Regime had never imagined: the **levee en masse**, the August 1793 mass conscription decree that called every French male between 18 and 25 to military service. By the end of 1794 France had about 1.5 million men under arms, the largest army in European history to that point. The professional armies of Austria and Prussia, which had topped out at perhaps 200,000 each, could not match the tide. The wars of the French Revolution and Napoleon would, for the next twenty-two years, be wars of mass armies fueled by patriotism, conscription, and ideology. The age of the levee en masse is the age in which "the nation in arms" replaces "the king's army." That is one of the longest-lived legacies of the Revolution.

#### Casualties of the Revolution: a count

<RechartsBar
  data={[
    { event: 'Bastille (1789)', deaths: 100 },
    { event: 'Sept Massacres (1792)', deaths: 1300 },
    { event: 'Terror, guillotine', deaths: 17000 },
    { event: 'Terror, prison + extrajudicial', deaths: 25000 },
    { event: 'Vendee (1793 to 1796)', deaths: 200000 },
    { event: 'Revolutionary Wars (French dead)', deaths: 600000 }
  ]}
  xKey="event"
  bars={[{ dataKey: 'deaths', name: 'Estimated deaths', color: '#b91c1c' }]}
  height={360}
/>

The Reign of Terror, the most famous chapter, accounts for roughly 5% of the decade's casualties. The Vendee civil war and the Revolutionary Wars together killed an order of magnitude more people, almost all of them outside Paris. The popular memory of the Revolution centers on the Place de la Revolution and the cart of condemned aristocrats; the actual demographic damage was suffered by peasant conscripts on the Rhine and by Catholic peasants in the marshes of the west.

### Significance

The Revolution killed the Old Regime in months, and made it impossible for any later European political order to ignore the demands of the third estate, of secularism, of equal application of the law. It also taught every later revolutionary movement, from Russia in 1917 to China in 1949, both the model for radical change and the cautionary tale of how that change can devour itself. The slogan **<RoughNotation type="underline" color="#b91c1c">"Liberty, Equality, Fraternity"</RoughNotation>** survived the Terror and is still the motto of the French Republic today. So is the date July 14: every Bastille Day for two centuries has been a national reaffirmation that the Republic is older than any of its current institutions.

The Revolution also set up the next twenty years of European history. Napoleon will spread Revolutionary ideas across the continent at the point of a bayonet (Era 4). The Congress of Vienna will try to bury those ideas in 1815 (Era 5). The 1848 revolutions will dig them back up. The unification of Italy and Germany in the 1860s and 1870s will be partly Revolutionary nationalism finally finishing the job. And the Russian Revolution of 1917, when the Bolsheviks consciously model themselves on the Jacobins of 1793, is the French Revolution's last great echo.

#### What survived after Thermidor

| What the Revolution killed permanently | What it almost killed but survived | What it created and we still use |
|---|---|---|
| Feudal privilege of the First and Second Estates | Catholic Church power in France (eventually restored under Napoleon's Concordat in 1801) | The motto Liberty, Equality, Fraternity |
| The legal status of "subject" in favor of "citizen" | The monarchy itself (Napoleon will crown himself in 1804) | The metric system |
| Tax exemptions of the clergy and nobility | Universal manhood suffrage (lost again until 1848) | The Declaration of the Rights of Man |
| The Estates-General as a representative body | Free press and free assembly (suspended repeatedly) | July 14 as a national holiday |
| Internal customs barriers between French provinces | Public education (Convention plans, executed under Napoleon) | The civil code framework that Napoleon will codify in 1804 |

The Revolution and the Terror together did not produce the durable Republic the Convention's deputies thought they were building. That took until the Third Republic in 1870 to finally stick. But they made the Old Regime, in its 1788 form, impossible to restore. Even the Restoration kings after 1815 had to govern under a constitution. Even Napoleon called himself Emperor of the French, not King of France, signaling a state that drew its legitimacy from popular plebiscite rather than divine right. After 1789, the language of legitimacy in Europe had changed permanently.

#### Recall checklist

- [ ] First Estate (clergy, 1% of pop, 10% of land, near zero taxes); Second Estate (nobles, 2% pop, 20% land, no taxes); Third Estate (97% of pop).
- [ ] Bread context: 3 to 4 loaves per family per day. Half of a worker's wages on bread.
- [ ] "Madame Deficit" was the popular nickname for Marie Antoinette.
- [ ] Estates-General convenes May 1789 for the first time in 175 years.
- [ ] Tennis Court Oath, June 20, 1789. Storming of the Bastille, July 14, 1789.
- [ ] Declaration of the Rights of Man and Citizen, August 26, 1789.
- [ ] Civil Constitution of the Clergy, July 1790: seizes Church land; alienates rural Catholic France.
- [ ] Louis XVI's failed flight to Varennes, June 1791, destroys public trust.
- [ ] September Massacres, September 1792. Convention abolishes the monarchy. Republic declared September 22, 1792.
- [ ] Louis XVI guillotined, January 21, 1793.
- [ ] Reign of Terror, June 1793 to July 1794. About 17,000 official executions. Robespierre's "Republic of Virtue."
- [ ] Vendee uprising, 1793 to 1796: about 200,000 dead in counter-revolutionary peasant war.
- [ ] Levee en masse, August 1793: mass conscription. France fields 1.5 million soldiers by 1794.
- [ ] Thermidorian Reaction, July 27, 1794. Robespierre guillotined July 28.
- [ ] The Directory, 1795 to 1799. Five-man executive plus two-house legislature.
- [ ] Emigres versus sans-culottes: counter-revolutionary nobles abroad versus working-class radicals in Paris.
- [ ] Napoleon's coup of 18 Brumaire, November 9, 1799, ends the Revolution. Era 4 begins.

{/* === ERA 4: NAPOLEON === */}

## 4. Napoleon, 1799 to 1815

> "I love power. But it is as an artist that I love it." Napoleon Bonaparte, in conversation with the artist David, c. 1808. Era 4: from a forgotten Corsican lieutenant to an emperor crowned by his own hand, then a prisoner of a rock in the South Atlantic. Sixteen years that taught Europe that revolution can wear a crown.

December 2, 1804. Notre-Dame Cathedral. Pope Pius VII has traveled from Rome through winter snow to anoint a new Catholic emperor. He stands at the altar, ready. Napoleon Bonaparte takes the laurel crown from the Pope's hands, lifts it, and places it on his own head. Then he crowns Josephine. The choreography is a message in stone: the Church can bless this throne, but it cannot grant it. Napoleon's authority comes from Napoleon. Five years earlier he was a thirty-year-old general overthrowing a corrupt directory in a half-bungled coup. Now he is the master of France and, soon, of most of continental Europe. He will rule for ten more years, redraw the map until it shatters, and die alone on a volcanic island four thousand miles from Paris.

<Callout type="insight" title="Why this matters">
  Napoleon is the hinge between revolution and reaction. He carries the French Revolution's reforms (legal equality, secular government, careers open to talent) at the point of a bayonet across Europe, even as he betrays the Revolution at home by crowning himself emperor. The Napoleonic Code outlives every battlefield. The map he draws is the map the Congress of Vienna will spend a year trying to unwrite.
</Callout>

### Cause: how a Corsican became First Consul

In late 1799, the Directory is dying. Five squabbling commissioners, runaway inflation, and a public so sick of revolution it would welcome almost anyone with the nerve to end it. Napoleon Bonaparte returns from a failed but well-publicized campaign in Egypt with two assets the Directory has lost: a reputation for victory, and a brother (Lucien) who happens to be president of the Council of Five Hundred. On <RoughNotation type="underline" color="#b91c1c">November 9, 1799</RoughNotation>, the <KeyTerm term="Coup d'etat">Coup d'etat</KeyTerm> of 18 Brumaire begins. Napoleon marches grenadiers into the legislative chamber at Saint-Cloud. Deputies climb out windows. Lucien rallies the loyal soldiers with a theatrical oath. By midnight, the Directory has been replaced by a three-man Consulate, and Napoleon is First Consul.

<Callout type="note" title="The plebiscite trick">
  Napoleon legitimized each step of his climb with a <KeyTerm term="Plebiscite">Plebiscite</KeyTerm>: a yes-or-no popular vote with one question on the ballot. Consul for life in 1802. Emperor in 1804. The vote always returned overwhelming approval, partly because the alternative was unclear, partly because the count was managed. The ritual mattered: every step pretended to be the will of the people.
</Callout>

### Effect: where it began to crack

Three mistakes brought down Napoleon's empire, and they compound one another. The first was economic, the second was Spanish, the third was Russian. Each was a refusal to accept that empire on Napoleon's scale required cooperation he could not coerce.

<Callout type="warning" title="Mistake 1, the Continental System">
  In November 1806 Napoleon decreed that no European port under his control could trade with Britain. The plan was to starve Britain of markets and force a negotiated peace. The plan failed for two reasons. First, Britain's <RoughNotation type="circle" color="#b91c1c">Royal Navy</RoughNotation> set up a counter-blockade, seizing neutral ships that traded with the Continent. Second, smuggling thrived: cotton, sugar, and coffee flowed in through Holland, Spain, Russia, and the Hanseatic ports. European economies depended on British trade more than they depended on French politics. The blockade injured France's allies more than it injured Britain.
</Callout>

In Spain, Napoleon installed his brother Joseph in 1808 over a Bourbon king the Spanish actually liked. The Spanish responded by inventing a new style of war. They called it the <KeyTerm term="Coup d'etat">guerrilla</KeyTerm>: small groups, irregular ambushes, no fixed lines, no surrender. The French could win every battle and still lose the country. The Peninsular War (1808 to 1814) drained roughly three hundred thousand French troops who could not be spared from the central front. Spain became, in Napoleon's own private phrase, "the Spanish ulcer."

### Russia 1812: the catastrophe

On June 24, 1812, Napoleon crosses the Niemen with 420,000 men. The Russians under Barclay de Tolly and Kutuzov refuse to fight. They retreat hundreds of miles, burning villages, crops, and grain stores. Napoleon advances into a devouring emptiness. **Scorched-earth tactics** deny him supply. At Borodino on September 7, Kutuzov finally stands and fights. About 70,000 dead and wounded in a single day. Napoleon wins the field but cannot finish the Russian army. He enters Moscow on September 14 to find it empty. Fires set by the departing governor consume two-thirds of the city in three days. Alexander does not negotiate. Napoleon waits five weeks for a peace offer that never comes, then begins the retreat in October along a route his own army has stripped bare. The temperature falls. Cossack cavalry harasses the column. Horses die first; then men. At the Berezina crossing, thousands drown or freeze. Of 420,000 who crossed the Niemen, about 10,000 trickle home.

### Significance: an emperor who was also a code

Napoleon's military empire collapsed within a single year of the Russian retreat. His political empire (the family kingdoms, the Continental System, the satellite states) was unwound by the Congress of Vienna. But the legal empire endured. The <KeyTerm term="Napoleonic Code">Napoleonic Code</KeyTerm>, with its commitments to civil equality, secular government, and protection of property, was kept by every state Napoleon had occupied. Belgium kept it. The Rhineland kept it. Italy kept it. Louisiana kept it. Quebec used it as a template. Latin American republics, after winning their independence in the 1820s, modeled their civil codes on the French. Two centuries later, the Code is still the foundation of the legal systems of about thirty countries. Napoleon was right that he was an artist of power. He was wrong about which medium would last. The campaigns are battlefield diagrams. The Code is a living document.

### You are Napoleon at Borodino

Imagine the morning of September 7, 1812. The Russian army has at last decided to fight. You have about 130,000 men against Kutuzov's 120,000. Your supplies are running low. Winter is six weeks away. Choose your move.

The empty victory describes what Russia was offering the entire campaign: ground for a price."
      },

        ending: "feintRetreat",
        historicalNote: "Counterfactual. A feigned retreat is one of Napoleon's signature moves earlier in his career (Austerlitz, 1805). At Borodino, it would not have worked.   I --> M
  M --> N[Abdication, exile to Elba]

{/* === ERA 5: VIENNA AND NATIONALISM === */}

## 5. Vienna and Nationalism, 1815 to 1871

> "When France sneezes, the rest of Europe catches cold." Prince Klemens von Metternich, in conversation, c. 1830. Era 5: after the Napoleonic typhoon, the great powers tried to put Europe back into the eighteenth century. The map redrew itself anyway, in the language of the nation-state.

September 1814. Vienna. The Habsburg capital is full of monarchs. The host is Prince Klemens von Metternich, foreign minister of Austria, fifty years old, the kind of man who wears his vanity like a uniform. He is selling a story: that the Revolution was a fever, that legitimate kings can wake up like nothing happened, that the eighteenth century can be restored if the right men carve the map carefully enough. The story holds for thirty years. It does not hold for fifty.

<Callout type="insight" title="Why this matters">
  The Congress of Vienna sets the European order for a generation by trying to stop time. It works long enough to give Europe forty years of relative peace, then cracks under nationalism and liberalism. By 1871 the map has been redrawn by those very ideas.
</Callout>

### Cause: Europe in 1815, exhausted and cold

Twenty-five years of war ended on June 18, 1815. The continent counted its dead and its empty treasuries. The four victorious powers (Britain, Prussia, Russia, Austria) plus a defeated but skillful France met in Vienna. Three ideas drove their work.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-accent-700">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-semibold mb-2">Goal 1</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Prevent French aggression</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Strengthen the states ringing France: enlarge the Netherlands to include Belgium, give the Rhineland to Prussia, restore Switzerland as a guaranteed neutral. France was returned to its 1792 borders, but ringed with stronger neighbors. France would not be punished further; it would be contained.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-ink-500">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-400 font-semibold mb-2">Goal 2</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Balance of power</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed"><KeyTerm term="Balance of power">Balance of power</KeyTerm>: no single state dominant on the continent. Russia gained most of Poland; Prussia got the Rhineland and parts of Saxony; Austria recovered northern Italy. France itself was preserved as a great power because removing it would have left only four major players, which Metternich considered unstable.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-accent-500">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-semibold mb-2">Goal 3</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Restore legitimacy</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed"><KeyTerm term="Legitimacy">Legitimacy</KeyTerm>: pre-Napoleonic dynasties returned to their thrones. The Bourbons in France and Spain, the Habsburgs in Austria, the Hohenzollerns in Prussia, dozens of smaller dynasties in Italian and German states. The principle was a fiction (many of these dynasties had been gone for years), but the fiction stabilized the map.</p>
  </div>
</div>

<Callout type="note" title="Metternich, the architect">
  Klemens von Metternich was Austria's foreign minister from 1809 to 1848, longer than most monarchs reigned. He understood that the Habsburg empire (a multi-ethnic patchwork of Germans, Hungarians, Czechs, Italians, Poles, Croats, Romanians, Serbs, Ukrainians, and Slovenes) could survive only if national feeling stayed asleep. Every Vienna decision was, partly, a defense of Vienna itself. The system collapsed in 1848. So did Metternich, who fled to England in disguise.
</Callout>

### Effect: nationalism wakes up anyway

The first two decades after Vienna looked like a triumph. Liberal uprisings in Spain in 1820 and Italy in 1820 to 1821 were crushed by Austrian troops sent under the cover of the <KeyTerm term="Concert of Europe">Concert of Europe</KeyTerm>. Greek independence (1821 to 1830) was the first major exception, and even that was a matter of British, French, and Russian intervention rather than spontaneous victory. Then came 1830. France replaced the inflexible Bourbon Charles X with his cousin Louis-Philippe, the citizen-king. Belgium broke off from the Netherlands. Polish revolutionaries rose against Russia and were brutally crushed. The Vienna order had cracked, but it had not yet broken.

In 1848 it broke. Within a single spring, Paris, Vienna, Berlin, Milan, Rome, Budapest, and Prague all rose. Metternich fled. Louis-Philippe abdicated. The German states held a parliament at Frankfurt and offered the Prussian king the crown of a unified Germany (he refused). Most uprisings were crushed within a year. But many gains stuck.

<BadFact source="every textbook that simplifies the 1840s, c. 1900" certainty="0%">
  The 1848 revolutions did NOT all fail outright. They achieved durable gains: serfdom was abolished in Austria-Hungary, the French Second Republic was established (briefly, before Louis-Napoleon's 1851 coup made him Napoleon III), constitutional reforms passed in Prussia and other German states, and parliamentary institutions strengthened across the continent. The "year of revolutions" looks like a failure only if you measure it by what the radicals demanded, not by what they pried loose.
</BadFact>

### Three political schools

After 1815, three visions competed: **Conservatives** (throne, altar, hierarchy; tradition over change; Metternich, Burke), **Liberals** (constitutional monarchy with limited suffrage, free markets, careers open to talent; Cavour, the British Whigs), and **Radicals** (democratic republic with universal male suffrage, full legal equality; the 1848 revolutionaries, Mazzini).

### Three kinds of nationalism

The most powerful new force was nationalism: loyalty to a community defined by shared language, history, religion, or territory. It came in three flavors, each with different implications for the map.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-semibold mb-2">Type 1</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Unification</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Merging culturally similar but politically scattered lands. <strong>Italy</strong> (about a dozen states unified by 1861, with Rome added in 1870). <strong>Germany</strong> (about 39 states unified under Prussia by 1871).</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-semibold mb-2">Type 2</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Separation</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Groups breaking away from multi-ethnic empires. Greek independence from the Ottomans (1830), Belgian independence from the Netherlands (1830 to 1839), later the Slavic and Balkan separations from Austria-Hungary and the Ottomans.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-semibold mb-2">Type 3</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">State-building</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Diverse populations forging a new nation around shared institutions rather than shared ethnicity. The classic case is the United States, where citizenship was civic and territorial. Latin American republics partly followed this model.</p>
  </div>
</div>

Each new flag was a defeat for Vienna: Belgium (1830), Greece (1830), Italy (1861), Germany (1871), Austria-Hungary (1867). Three multi-ethnic empires sat across the path of nationalism. **Austria-Hungary** (1867) split sovereignty with Hungary, buying fifty years and seeding the resentments that exploded at Sarajevo. **Russia** tried Russification, which intensified separatist nationalism rather than dissolving it. The **Ottomans** watched Greek, Serbian, Bulgarian, and Romanian separations carve away their European provinces.

### Italy and Germany unify

By the 1860s, the unifying nationalist movements had two engines. In <strong>Italy</strong>, Camillo Cavour, the prime minister of the small but well-administered Kingdom of Piedmont-Sardinia, allied with France against Austria, used Garibaldi's volunteer Red Shirts to take Sicily and Naples, and assembled the Kingdom of Italy under King Victor Emmanuel II by 1861. Rome was added in 1870 when French troops were withdrawn during the Franco-Prussian War.

In <strong>Germany</strong>, Otto von Bismarck used three short, decisive wars (against Denmark in 1864, Austria in 1866, France in 1870 to 1871) to unify the German states under Prussian dominance. The German Empire was proclaimed on January 18, 1871, in the Hall of Mirrors at Versailles, the same room where Louis XIV had glittered two centuries earlier. The choice was deliberate humiliation. Bismarck had built the most powerful state in Europe by hard-headed manipulation of crises and alliances. The new Germany inherited his methods.

### You are Bismarck after 1871

Imagine yourself in Berlin in early 1872. The German Empire has been proclaimed in the Hall of Mirrors a year earlier. France has been defeated, Alsace-Lorraine annexed, the Empire's federal constitution drafted. Now what? You can pick alliances, build forces, attack neighbors, or build colonies. Each move shapes the next forty years.

### Significance: nationalism becomes the master ideology

The Vienna settlement intended to suppress nationalism. Instead, by giving the great powers a generation of peace, it gave national movements a generation to mature. By 1871, the political map of Europe was the map of <KeyTerm term="Nation-state">nation-states</KeyTerm>: not a perfect alignment of state and nation, but a closer one than at any time since the Reformation. The five great powers of 1815 (Britain, France, Russia, Austria, Prussia) had become five different powers of 1871. Britain remained, more dominant in trade. Germany rose to the top of continental power. Austria-Hungary survived only by halving itself. Russia retreated from Europe-shaping ambitions for a generation after the Crimean defeat. France, although still a great power, no longer set the European agenda.

The deeper effect was a long-running tension: liberal ideas (constitutions, suffrage, civil rights) and nationalist ideas (a state for our people) were sometimes allies, sometimes rivals. They were allies in the Greek and Italian risings, where liberal nationalists fought together. They were rivals when nationalism turned ethnic, exclusive, and willing to use the state's coercive power against minority populations. The contradiction would matter more, not less, in the next century.

### Causal flowchart

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  A[Napoleon defeated 1815] --> B[Congress of Vienna]
  B --> C[Three goals: prevent French aggression, balance of power, legitimacy]
  C --> D[Conservative Concert of Europe]
  D --> E[Crackdown on uprisings 1820, 1830]
  E --> F[Industrialization + middle class growth]
  F --> G[1848 Revolutions]
  G --> H[Most uprisings crushed]
  G --> I[Durable gains: serfdom abolished, parliaments strengthened]
  C --> J[Nationalism wakes: unification, separation, state-building]
  J --> K[Italian unification 1861 to 1870]
  J --> L[German unification 1864 to 1871]
  J --> M[Austria-Hungary 1867 dual monarchy]
  J --> N[Russification backfires]
  L --> O[New balance of power 1871: Britain + Germany rise; Austria + Russia decline]
  K --> O
```

{/* === ERA 6: INDUSTRIAL REVOLUTION === */}

## 6. Industrial Revolution, 1750 to 1900

> "Workers of the world, unite. You have nothing to lose but your chains." Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels, The Communist Manifesto, February 1848. Era 6: machines replace muscle, factories replace cottages, slums replace fields, and a new ideology forms in the cities to argue about who owns the new wealth.

Manchester, 1820. A nine-year-old child stands on a wooden platform under a cotton-spinning machine. Her job, called a piecer, is to crawl beneath the moving frame and tie together the broken threads. The machine never stops. The shift is fourteen hours, six days a week. The air is thick with cotton dust. By the time she is sixteen, if she lives that long, her lungs will be permanently scarred. Her mother also worked in the mill. Her father is a loom mechanic. Her grandparents farmed in Lancashire until the enclosures of the 1790s sold off the common land they had grazed. There was no work in the village. There is work here. Her village in 1750 had four hundred people. Manchester in 1820 has 130,000 and is climbing past 300,000 by 1850. She has never seen a tree she could climb.

<Callout type="insight" title="Why this matters">
  The Industrial Revolution rewrote three things at once: the means of production (from hand to machine), the geography of population (from village to city), and the social order (from estates to classes). The wealth it generated paid for ironclad navies, transcontinental railroads, and modern medicine, but the bottom 70 percent of the population paid for it first, in lives shortened by decades. Every later European story (imperialism, World War I, Russian Revolution, the welfare state, the Cold War) is partly a reckoning with the Industrial Revolution's bargain.
</Callout>

### Timeline of industrial milestones

A scroll-revealed alternating timeline of the inventions, infrastructure, and ideas that built industrial Europe.

    { date: "1851", title: "Britain becomes majority-urban", subtitle: "First country in history", body: "The 1851 census shows more Britons living in cities than in rural areas. The Great Exhibition at the Crystal Palace announces British industrial supremacy to the world.", icon: "🏙" },

  ]}
/>

### Cause: why Britain went first

Industrialization was not inevitable. It happened in Britain first because Britain had a particular combination of advantages no other country fully shared.

<Callout type="note" title="The factor stack">
  <strong>Land:</strong> abundant <KeyTerm term="Factors of production">coal and iron ore</KeyTerm>, navigable rivers, deep harbors, and a temperate, wet climate suited to cotton spinning. <strong>Labor:</strong> the <KeyTerm term="Agricultural Revolution">Agricultural Revolution</KeyTerm> (enclosures, crop rotation, selective breeding) had increased food output and freed surplus rural labor for new factories. <strong>Capital:</strong> two centuries of trade with India, the Caribbean, and the Americas had built up wealth in the hands of London merchants and provincial banks willing to fund risky new ventures. <strong>Politics:</strong> the Glorious Revolution had stabilized property rights, low domestic tariffs, and a parliament friendly to entrepreneurs. <strong>Geography:</strong> island access to overseas markets and raw materials, plus an empire that could absorb manufactured goods.
</Callout>

The textile industry industrialized first. Demand for cotton cloth ran ahead of any cottage producer's capacity. The Spinning Jenny, Water Frame, Power Loom, and Cotton Gin each closed a bottleneck and created a new one further down the line. Production left the cottage and concentrated in <KeyTerm term="Factory system">factory</KeyTerm> buildings near rivers, then later near coalfields when steam replaced water as the main power source.

#### The takeoff curve

UK coal and pig-iron output, plus rail kilometres laid, on a single time axis. Notice how rail explodes after 1830 and pulls coal and iron up with it. The curves are not linear; industrial growth compounded.

### Effect: a new social class structure

Industrial society sorted itself into three layers, and the layer at the top was new.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-accent-700">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-semibold mb-2">Top, ~5 percent</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Industrial Upper Class</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Factory owners, mine owners, railway barons, banking houses. Wealth surpassed the old landed aristocracy by mid-century. They built mansions in suburbs upwind of the smokestacks, sat in Parliament, and increasingly intermarried with the gentry whose money they had outpaced.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-ink-500">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-400 font-semibold mb-2">Middle, ~25 percent</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Middle Class</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Professionals, merchants, managers, clerks, doctors, lawyers. Comfortable but conscious of their distance from both the wealthy and the poor. Built terraced houses with servants, sent sons to grammar schools, valued respectability, sobriety, savings. The political base of nineteenth-century liberalism.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-amber-600">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-amber-700 dark:text-amber-500 font-semibold mb-2">Bottom, ~70 percent</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Working Class</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Factory operatives, miners, dockworkers, domestic servants. Long hours, dangerous conditions, fragile budgets. Lived in tenements, often without running water. The first generations died young in cities that gradually learned to keep them alive. The political base of nineteenth- and twentieth-century socialism and labor.</p>
  </div>
</div>

### Significance: five economic theories born in the smoke

The Industrial Revolution did not just produce machines and money. It produced an argument about how human beings should organize the production of wealth. Five major theories took shape between 1776 and 1848.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-accent-500">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-semibold mb-2">1776</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Laissez-faire</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Government should not interfere in the economy. Self-interest, competition, and supply and demand together produce wealth as if guided by an invisible hand. Argued by Adam Smith in <em>The Wealth of Nations</em>, 1776.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-accent-700">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-semibold mb-2">~1800</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Capitalism</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Private ownership of the means of production, market exchange, and profit-driven investment. The dominant economic system of the industrial nineteenth century. Lifted overall output but left the distribution of gains highly unequal.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-emerald-600">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-emerald-700 dark:text-emerald-400 font-semibold mb-2">~1820</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Utilitarianism</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Policies should produce the greatest happiness for the greatest number. Argued by Jeremy Bentham and refined by John Stuart Mill. Used to justify factory inspections, prison reform, and public health Acts.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-blue-600">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-blue-700 dark:text-blue-400 font-semibold mb-2">~1825</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Socialism</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">Public or cooperative ownership of the means of production. Aimed to reduce inequality without abolishing markets entirely. Robert Owen, Saint-Simon, Charles Fourier. Owen ran a model factory at New Lanark with shorter hours, schools, and decent housing, and tried (and failed) to build a utopian community in Indiana.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-red-700">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-red-700 dark:text-red-400 font-semibold mb-2">1848</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Marxism / Communism</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">A radical form of socialism. Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels argued in <em>The Communist Manifesto</em> (1848) that industrial society had produced two classes (bourgeoisie and proletariat) whose conflict would end in revolution and a classless society. Became the official ideology of the Soviet Union after 1917.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="surface rounded-xl shadow-soft p-5 border-l-4 border-amber-600">
    <div class="text-[10px] uppercase tracking-[0.16em] text-amber-700 dark:text-amber-400 font-semibold mb-2">Trade-offs</div>
    <h4 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold mb-3">Where they meet</h4>
    <p class="text-sm leading-relaxed">In practice, every industrial society borrowed from several theories at once. Britain combined laissez-faire rhetoric with Factory Acts in fact. Germany, under Bismarck, combined a capitalist economy with the first state social-insurance system. The Soviet Union after 1917 attempted full Marxism. The history of the next century is the history of these mixes.</p>
  </div>
</div>

### Causal flowchart: from steam to social theory

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  A[Britain's factor stack: coal, iron, capital, labor, ports] --> B[Agricultural Revolution: enclosure, surplus food, displaced labor]
  B --> C[Textile inventions: Spinning Jenny, Water Frame, Power Loom, Cotton Gin]
  C --> D[Factory system replaces cottage industry]
  D --> E[Steam power, coal-based]
  E --> F[Railroads and steamships, 1804 to 1825]
  F --> G[National and global markets]
  D --> H[Urbanization: Manchester, Birmingham, Liverpool]
  H --> I[Tenements, disease, life expectancy below 20 in slums]
  H --> J[Working class becomes 70 percent of population]
  J --> K[Labor unions, strikes, collective bargaining]
  K --> L[Factory Acts, Mines Act, Public Health Act]
  J --> M[Five economic theories: laissez-faire, capitalism, utilitarianism, socialism, Marxism]
  M --> N[Reform movements: abolition 1833 / 1865, women's rights 1888, education]
  G --> O[Demand for raw materials and markets]
  O --> P[New Imperialism, 1870 onward, see Era 7]
```

The popular caricature came from later industrialist propaganda."

{/* === START OF ERA 7: IMPERIALISM === */}

## Era 7: Imperialism, 1870 to 1914

<Callout type="warning" title="Primary source: read as evidence, not endorsement">
  The pull quote below is taken from Rudyard Kipling's 1899 poem "The White Man's Burden." It is taught here as a primary source artifact, the kind of imperial propaganda Europeans used to dress conquest as moral duty. We quote it so you can analyze it. We do not endorse it.
</Callout>

> "Take up the White Man's burden." Rudyard Kipling, 1899. The line that gave Europeans a self-flattering vocabulary for empire, and that still poisons the way we talk about that century.

### The arc on a vertical timeline

<VerticalTimeline items={[
  { date: "1830", title: "France invades Algeria", body: "Opening shot of New Imperialism. France treats Algeria as part of metropolitan France, not as a colony.", icon: "F" },
  { date: "1857", title: "Sepoy Mutiny", body: "Indian troops in the British East India Company army revolt over greased cartridges. Crushed by 1858.", icon: "I" },
  { date: "1858", title: "British Crown takes India", body: "Parliament dissolves the East India Company and installs direct Crown rule. The Raj begins.", icon: "C" },
  { date: "1869", title: "Suez Canal opens", body: "Egyptian and French engineering links the Mediterranean to the Red Sea. London to Bombay shrinks by weeks.", icon: "S" },
  { date: "1879", title: "Leopold II hires Stanley", body: "Belgium's king begins his private project to control the Congo basin under cover of a humanitarian society.", icon: "L" },
  { date: "1882", title: "Britain occupies Egypt", body: "After a nationalist revolt, British troops take Cairo. Egypt becomes a protectorate in all but name.", icon: "E" },
  { date: "1884", title: "Berlin Conference opens", body: "Bismarck hosts fourteen European nations to set rules for African claims. No African delegates present.", icon: "B" },
  { date: "1885", title: "Berlin Conference ends", body: "The General Act sets effective occupation as the rule for valid claims. Triggers the Scramble.", icon: "B" },
  { date: "1896", title: "Ethiopia defeats Italy at Adwa", body: "Menelik II's army routs the Italian invasion. Ethiopia stays independent for four more decades.", icon: "A" },
  { date: "1898", title: "Fashoda Incident", body: "British and French expeditions meet in the Sudan. War almost breaks out, then France backs down.", icon: "F" },
  { date: "1899", title: "Kipling publishes 'White Man's Burden'", body: "An English-language slogan for the next generation of imperial apologetics.", icon: "K" },
  { date: "1908", title: "Belgium takes the Congo from Leopold", body: "After international outcry over forced rubber labor and millions of deaths, Belgium nationalizes Leopold's private colony.", icon: "C" },
  { date: "1914", title: "About 90 percent of Africa colonized", body: "On the eve of WWI, only Ethiopia and Liberia remain independent on the continent.", icon: "9" }
]} />

### Cause: factories, flags, and a borrowed racial theory

<KeyTerm term="Imperialism">The policy of extending a country's authority over foreign territory, by conquest, settlement, or economic and political domination. New Imperialism (1870 to 1914) was driven by industrial demand for raw materials and markets, national prestige, and the technological gap with the colonized.</KeyTerm>

By 1870, Europe is the workshop of the world. British factories want American cotton and Indian indigo and Malayan rubber and Congolese ivory. German firms want copper for electrical wire. French banks want somewhere to send the capital they have piled up. The metropole has run out of cheap labor and cheap land at home. It has not run out of ambition. So the search for raw materials, captive markets, and prestige all push in the same direction: outward.

But there is one more piece. Europeans had spent the last century telling themselves a flattering story about why their machines worked. The story called itself science.

<KeyTerm term="Social Darwinism">A misapplication of Darwin's natural selection to human societies, arguing that some races and nations were biologically superior and destined to dominate. Used to justify imperialism and racism.</KeyTerm>

<KeyTerm term="Paternalism">The colonial doctrine that subject peoples were like children needing European guidance, education, and Christianization. Used to make exploitation sound like moral duty.</KeyTerm>

<Callout type="warning" title="Two stories Europeans told themselves">
  Social Darwinism said European victory was nature's verdict, the strong dominating the weak. Paternalism said European victory was a moral duty, the strong owing protection to the weak. Both stories solved the same problem: how do you square exploitation with the Enlightenment ideal of universal human dignity? You don't. You redefine "human" so it does not include the people you want to exploit.
</Callout>

### Four motives, one acronym

The motives behind New Imperialism are classically grouped into four buckets that almost spell something memorable. Use whichever ordering helps you remember:

<figcaption class="font-serif text-base font-semibold mb-2">Why Europeans built empires (the four-motives stack)</figcaption>
<RechartsBar
  data={[
    { motive: "Economic", weight: 40 },
    { motive: "Political", weight: 25 },
    { motive: "Social/Cultural", weight: 25 },
    { motive: "Exploratory", weight: 10 }
  ]}
  xKey="motive"
  bars={[{ dataKey: "weight", name: "Relative weight" }]}
  height={360}
/>

<Callout type="tip" title="The four motives in plain English">
  **Economic.** Industrial Europe needs rubber, cotton, oil, ivory, palm oil, copper, tin, and cheap labor. It also needs markets to sell finished goods back to. **Political.** Empires equal national greatness. A Prussian who would not have dreamed of South Pacific atolls in 1800 wants German New Guinea by 1885. **Social/Cultural.** Social Darwinism plus paternalism plus missionary Christianity equal a self-justifying ideology. **Exploratory.** A genuine if smaller motive: Stanley, Livingstone, the search for the source of the Nile. Often the cover story for the other three.
</Callout>

### Effect: the Berlin Conference and the cake

In 1880 only about ten percent of Africa is under European rule. Most of that is coastal: French Algeria, British Cape Colony, Portuguese trading posts. The interior is independent. African armies have successfully fought off European armies for centuries. Tropical disease (malaria especially) has killed Europeans faster than Europeans could replace them. Then, in roughly thirty years, that ten percent becomes ninety.

The catalyst is a meeting in Berlin in the winter of 1884 to 1885.

<KeyTerm term="Berlin Conference">November 1884 to February 1885. European powers, hosted by Bismarck, partitioned Africa among themselves with no African delegates present. Drew borders that ignored ethnic and linguistic boundaries; consequences run for over a century.</KeyTerm>

<DeepZoomAnnotated
  title="Colonial Africa, 1913"
  src="https://images.weserv.nl/?url=commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Special:FilePath/Colonial_Africa_1913_map.svg&w=1600&output=png"
  height={520}
  markers={[
    { x: 0.42, y: 0.18, label: "French North Africa", description: "Algeria (1830, settler colony treated as part of France itself), Tunisia (1881 protectorate), and the Moroccan protectorate (1912)." },
    { x: 0.65, y: 0.18, label: "British Egypt", description: "Protectorate after 1882. The Suez Canal makes Egypt strategic gold for the route to India." },
    { x: 0.55, y: 0.50, label: "Belgian Congo", description: "Leopold II's personal property until 1908, then formally Belgium's. Rubber-extraction regime; estimates of forced-labor deaths run as high as 10 million." },
    { x: 0.72, y: 0.40, label: "Ethiopia", description: "One of only two African states never colonized in this era. Menelik II defeats Italy at Adwa, 1896. Stays independent until Mussolini's invasion in 1935." },

    { x: 0.55, y: 0.85, label: "South Africa (UK)", description: "Cape Colony plus the former Boer republics (Transvaal, Orange Free State) after the 1899-1902 Boer War. Settler-dominated, gold-rich." }
  ]}
/>

#### See it as a globe: imperial sea-lanes, c. 1900

Drag to spin. The arcs are the sea-lanes that fed industrial Europe: cotton from Bombay to Liverpool, rubber from the Congo to Antwerp, gold from the Witwatersrand to London, sugar from Cuba and Java to Amsterdam, oil after 1900 from Baku and the Persian Gulf. The pulses are not just trade lines; they are imperial life-support cables. Cut one, the metropole feels it within weeks.

      svg: `<svg viewBox="0 0 400 240" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg"><rect width="400" height="240" fill="#fffbe5" /><g stroke="#000" stroke-width="2" fill="none"><circle cx="120" cy="120" r="10" /><line x1="120" y1="130" x2="120" y2="160" /><circle cx="160" cy="120" r="10" /><line x1="160" y1="130" x2="160" y2="160" /><circle cx="200" cy="120" r="10" /><line x1="200" y1="130" x2="200" y2="160" /><circle cx="240" cy="120" r="10" /><line x1="240" y1="130" x2="240" y2="160" /><circle cx="280" cy="120" r="10" /><line x1="280" y1="130" x2="280" y2="160" /></g><line x1="180" y1="40" x2="180" y2="200" stroke="#b91c1c" stroke-width="4" stroke-dasharray="6 4" /><text x="180" y="225" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="14" fill="#b91c1c">colonial border</text><text x="200" y="30" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="14">one ethnic group, two countries</text></svg>`
    },
    {
      duration: 5,
      caption: "Decades of conflict follow. Some of those borders are still drawn the same way today.",
      svg: `<svg viewBox="0 0 400 240" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg"><rect width="400" height="240" fill="#fffbe5" /><text x="200" y="80" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="20" font-weight="bold">1885 to today</text><text x="200" y="115" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="14">borders unchanged</text><text x="200" y="140" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="14">conflicts unresolved</text><line x1="80" y1="170" x2="320" y2="170" stroke="#b91c1c" stroke-width="3" /><text x="200" y="200" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="14" fill="#b91c1c">consequences run for over a century</text></svg>`
    }
  ]}
  loop={true}
/>

The Berlin Conference set one rule that mattered above all: **effective occupation**. To claim a piece of African territory, a European power had to actually be on the ground, with troops or administrators or settlers. That single rule turned the Scramble from a slow drift into a sprint.

<BadFact title="Berlin did not actually draw the borders">
  Common simplification: "the Berlin Conference of 1884 to 1885 partitioned Africa." That is not quite right. The Conference did not in itself draw most colonial borders. What it did was set the rules of the game: free navigation on the Niger and Congo rivers, an effective-occupation requirement for valid claims, and a polite way for powers to recognize each others' spheres without going to war. Most actual partition happened in the years after, through bilateral treaties, military expeditions, and treaties with African chiefs (often signed under coercion or misrepresentation). Berlin was the rulebook, not the map.
</BadFact>

### Significance: why this era matters

<KeyTerm term="Berlin Conference">November 1884 to February 1885. European powers, hosted by Bismarck, partitioned Africa among themselves with no African delegates present. Drew borders that ignored ethnic and linguistic boundaries; consequences run for over a century.</KeyTerm>

Three significances stack up. First, **imperialism remade the global economy**: Europe became the workshop, the rest of the world the resource basket and the market. That economic geography lasted until decolonization in the mid-twentieth century, and its echoes are still visible in trade flows today. Second, **the colonial borders drawn between 1884 and 1914 are mostly the borders that still exist on the map**. African civil wars, ethnic conflicts, and statelessness in the late twentieth century happen on borders drawn by Europeans who had never been there. Third, **imperial rivalry fed the alliance system that produced WWI**. Britain and France had nearly gone to war over Fashoda in the Sudan in 1898. Germany felt boxed out of the partition and demanded "a place in the sun." The colonial competitions trained European foreign ministries to think in terms of zero-sum great-power blocs. When the Sarajevo crisis hit in 1914, the diplomatic muscle memory that responded had been built at Berlin.

### Mermaid: the cause-effect chain

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  A[Industrial Revolution: factories need raw materials and markets] --> B[Tech edge: machine guns, steamships, quinine, telegraphs]
  C[Social Darwinism + paternalism + missionary Christianity] --> D[Story that justifies conquest]
  E[Nationalism: empire equals national greatness] --> D
  B --> F[European armies can now penetrate African interior]
  D --> F
  F --> G[Berlin Conference 1884-85: rules for the partition]
  G --> H[Scramble for Africa: 90% colonized in 30 years]
  H --> I[Artificial borders, forced labor, cultural disruption]
  H --> J[Imperial rivalries train European foreign ministries]
  J --> K[Alliance blocs harden: Triple Alliance vs Triple Entente]
  K --> L[1914: alliance system activates after Sarajevo]
```

### Primary source corner

<DocAnnotate
  title="Kipling, 'The White Man's Burden,' 1899 (excerpt)"
  text={`Take up the White Man's burden,
Send forth the best ye breed,
Go bind your sons to exile,
To serve your captives' need;
To wait, in heavy harness,
On fluttered folk and wild,
Your new-caught, sullen peoples,
Half devil and half child.`}
/>

<Callout type="note" title="Reading the poem">
Four phrases do most of the work. **"White Man's burden"** frames imperial conquest as a moral duty rather than economic exploitation: it is the slogan that gave a generation of Europeans and Americans a way to feel virtuous about empire. **"captives' need"** is paternalist sleight of hand: the colonized are recast as people whose own needs require European service, when the actual labor relationship is the reverse. **"fluttered folk and wild"** is dehumanizing language; the poem's argument depends on its readers accepting that the colonized are not fully their equals. **"Half devil and half child"** packages the two stock figures of imperial rhetoric: the colonized as either dangerous savage or helpless infant. Both stories solve the same problem, denying full humanity while claiming to act on its behalf.
</Callout>

<Callout type="warning" title="Reading like a historian, not like Kipling">
  This poem is what historians call a primary source. Your job with it is to identify the rhetorical work it does, not to be moved by it. Kipling published it in 1899, the year the United States annexed the Philippines. Its immediate audience was Americans. Its argument, in plain English: "join us in this conquest, the moral cost will feel heavy, but the burden is yours and you must carry it." Read it the way you would read a recruiting pamphlet.
</Callout>

{/* === START OF ERA 8: WORLD WAR I === */}

## Era 8: World War I, 1914 to 1918

> "The lamps are going out all over Europe; we shall not see them lit again in our lifetime." Edward Grey, British Foreign Secretary, the evening of August 3, 1914. Less than twenty-four hours later Britain declared war on Germany.

### Cause: the four MAIN causes

The textbook acronym is **MAIN**: Militarism, Alliances, Imperialism, Nationalism. Each is loaded; together they made a regional crisis impossible to contain.

<Collapsible title="M is for Militarism">
Glorification of military power and the maintenance of large standing armies. Germany doubled its army between 1880 and 1914. Britain and Germany ran a naval-shipbuilding race centered on the dreadnought battleship. Generals had detailed mobilization timetables and very little flexibility once they were activated. The military culture of pre-1914 Europe assumed a war was coming and that being unprepared was the worst sin.
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible title="A is for Alliances">
Europe split into two armed blocs. Triple Alliance (1882): Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy. Triple Entente (1907): France, Russia, Great Britain. Treaty obligations meant a war between any two great powers could pull in the rest. Sarajevo activated all of it within five weeks.
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible title="I is for Imperialism">
Decades of colonial rivalry trained European foreign ministries to treat each other as zero-sum competitors. Britain and France nearly went to war over Fashoda in 1898. Germany resented being late to the partition of Africa. The Russo-Japanese War of 1904 to 1905 humiliated Russia. Imperial competition made every diplomatic crisis feel like a test of national prestige.
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible title="N is for Nationalism">
Pride and loyalty to one's nation, often expressed as hostility toward others. Pan-Slavic nationalism in Serbia. Pan-German nationalism in Berlin. Revanchist French nationalism wanting Alsace-Lorraine back from Germany after 1871. The Balkans had a dozen ethnic groups inside three multi-ethnic empires (Ottoman, Austro-Hungarian, Russian). Nationalism made every crisis emotional.
</Collapsible>

<KeyTerm term="Triple Alliance">The pre-WWI defensive alliance of Germany, Austria-Hungary, and Italy (signed 1882). Italy switched sides in 1915 and joined the Allies; Germany and Austria-Hungary fought as the Central Powers.</KeyTerm>

<KeyTerm term="Triple Entente">The pre-WWI loose alliance of France, Russia, and Britain, formalized between 1894 and 1907. Counterweight to the Triple Alliance and the framework of the WWI Allied side.</KeyTerm>

#### See the alliance system

The treaties were a web, not a list. Drag any node to untangle. The clusters tell the story: Central Powers in red, Entente in dark, Italy already drifting (it would switch in 1915), Serbia tied to Russia by Pan-Slav sentiment, Belgium pulled in by the British guarantee of 1839 the moment Germany violated it.

### The domino falls

#### The 37-day chain in 7 clicks

Each click is one cable, one ultimatum, or one declaration. By the end you have walked from a single act of political assassination to a six-power continental war, in five weeks.

### First Battle of the Marne and the trench

The German advance came within forty miles of Paris. The French government evacuated to Bordeaux. Then, in early September 1914, the French Sixth Army (reportedly transported in part by Paris taxicabs) and the British Expeditionary Force counterattacked north of Paris along the Marne River. The Germans, exhausted, overextended, and short of supplies, were pushed back about forty miles and dug in. The First Battle of the Marne was the most consequential battle of 1914. It saved Paris. It killed the Schlieffen Plan. And by stopping a war of movement, it created the war of position that would define the next four years.

<KeyTerm term="Trench warfare">The static, attrition-style warfare that dominated the Western Front from 1914 to 1918. Two parallel lines of trenches separated by no man's land, machine guns, barbed wire, and artillery. Produced casualties on a scale never before seen.</KeyTerm>
