  <TremorCard label="Berlin Airlift tonnage" value="2.3M tons" helper="Food, coal, supplies into West Berlin by air alone." />
  <TremorCard label="Marshall Plan total" value="12.5B USD" helper="About 175 billion in 2024 dollars." />
  <TremorCard label="Peak nuclear stockpile, 1986" value="70,000" helper="Combined U.S. and Soviet warheads." />
  <TremorCard label="U.S. defense, peak Cold War" value="6% GDP" helper="Reagan-era buildup; today about 3 percent." />
  <TremorCard label="Iran Hostage Crisis" value="444 days" helper="Released the hour Reagan was inaugurated." />
</div>

#### The Space Race

Competition extended to outer space. In **October 1957**, the USSR launched **Sputnik**, the first artificial satellite. The U.S. responded with frantic investment in science and engineering education (the National Defense Education Act of 1958), the founding of NASA, and Project Mercury. The Soviets put the first man in space (Yuri Gagarin, April 1961). The U.S. landed men on the Moon (Apollo 11, July 1969). The technological yield (rocketry, transistors, computer networking, satellite communications) eventually changed every part of the global economy.

#### The U-2 incident

In **May 1960**, just before a planned summit between Eisenhower and Khrushchev in Paris, a U-2 spy plane piloted by Francis Gary Powers was shot down over Sverdlovsk in the Soviet Union. Eisenhower initially denied that the U.S. ran U-2 flights over Soviet territory; Khrushchev then produced both the wreckage and the captured pilot. The Paris summit collapsed. The thaw of the late 1950s collapsed with it.

#### Cuba and the missile crisis

Cuba was ruled by Fulgencio Batista, a U.S.-backed dictator, until 1959. **Fidel Castro** led a revolution and overthrew Batista that year. Castro's first international moves were nationalist; his alignment was not yet clear. When Castro nationalized U.S.-owned sugar mills, Eisenhower imposed an embargo. Castro turned to the Soviets for economic and military aid. By 1960 Cuba was firmly in the Soviet orbit.

In **April 1961**, three months into Kennedy's presidency, the CIA launched the **Bay of Pigs** invasion: about 1,400 Cuban exiles, trained and equipped by the U.S., landed on the southern Cuban coast. The plan depended on a popular uprising that did not happen and on U.S. air support that Kennedy refused to provide. The invasion was crushed within three days. The U.S. looked weak, Castro looked stronger, and Khrushchev concluded that Kennedy was a man who could be bullied.

In **October 1962**, U-2 photos confirmed Soviet medium-range ballistic missile installations under construction in Cuba. For thirteen days, the **Cuban Missile Crisis** played out in secrecy and in public. Kennedy imposed a naval **quarantine** (technically a blockade is an act of war; "quarantine" was a euphemism designed to leave political room). Soviet ships approached the line and turned back. Behind the scenes, Robert Kennedy met with the Soviet ambassador Anatoly Dobrynin to broker a deal: the Soviets would remove the missiles from Cuba; the United States would publicly pledge not to invade Cuba and would (this part secret for decades) remove its Jupiter missiles from Turkey.

<KeyTerm term="Cuban Missile Crisis">October 1962. Thirteen days during which the U.S. discovered Soviet nuclear missiles in Cuba and imposed a naval quarantine. Kennedy and Khrushchev stepped back from nuclear war via secret negotiation. The closest the Cold War came to going hot.</KeyTerm>

#### Walk it: thirteen days as a scrolly story

#### Vietnam: spot the Vietcong

The Cold War's longest hot proxy was Vietnam. American patrols learned, slowly and at terrible cost, that the dense jungle hid an enemy who chose every encounter. Try it from a Marine's POV.

<JungleSpot />

### Detente and renewed tension

By the late 1960s, the costs of brinkmanship had become unmistakable. The Sino-Soviet split (the USSR cut aid and refused to share nuclear technology with China in 1959; relations deteriorated through the 1960s into border clashes) opened space for U.S. diplomacy with both Moscow and Beijing.

**Richard Nixon** and his National Security Advisor **Henry Kissinger** practiced **realpolitik**, decision-making based on practical power balance rather than ideology. Nixon visited China in February 1972 (the famous "only Nixon could go to China") and the USSR three months later, signing the **SALT I** treaty limiting strategic nuclear weapons. The decade of **detente** ran roughly from 1969 to 1979. Other detente-era achievements: the Helsinki Accords (1975), the Apollo-Soyuz Test Project (1975), routinized arms-control negotiations.

<KeyTerm term="Detente">French for relaxation. The 1970s easing of U.S.-Soviet tensions through arms control treaties (SALT I in 1972), the Helsinki Accords (1975), and a hotline. Ended after the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan in 1979.</KeyTerm>

Detente ended around 1979. The Soviet invasion of **Afghanistan** in December 1979, the Iranian Revolution and its disruption of Persian Gulf oil supplies, and the election of **Ronald Reagan** in November 1980 closed the era. Reagan ramped up U.S. defense spending sharply, denounced the USSR as an "evil empire" in 1983, and announced the **Strategic Defense Initiative (SDI)** in 1983, a proposed missile-defense system. SDI was widely derided as "Star Wars" and never fully developed, but it (along with U.S. missile deployments in Western Europe and the broader rearmament) put pressure on a Soviet economy that could not match the spending.

### "You are Gorbachev in 1989"

Step into the Kremlin in summer 1989. Eastern Europe is sliding. The economy is broken. The army is restless. What do you do?

### The collapse: 1989 to 1991

**1985:** Mikhail Gorbachev becomes General Secretary of the Communist Party. He launches **glasnost** ("openness," easing censorship and political prisoners) and **perestroika** ("restructuring," economic liberalization including local management of farms and factories, foreign investment, partial market mechanisms). He also pushes **democratization** within the party, allowing competitive internal elections.

<KeyTerm term="Glasnost">Russian for openness. Gorbachev's mid-1980s policy of permitting public criticism, freer press, and disclosure of Soviet crimes. Loosened the grip of censorship and surfaced grievances the regime could no longer contain.</KeyTerm>

<KeyTerm term="Perestroika">Russian for restructuring. Gorbachev's mid-1980s economic reforms that introduced limited market mechanisms into the Soviet economy. Failed to revive growth and accelerated the system's collapse.</KeyTerm>

In **December 1987**, Reagan and Gorbachev signed the **INF Treaty** (Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces), eliminating an entire class of land-based nuclear missiles in Europe. The arms race began to wind down.

In **1989**, the satellite empire collapsed in a wave: partially free elections in Poland (June), border opening between Hungary and Austria (May), mass protests in Leipzig and Berlin (October to November), the **fall of the Berlin Wall** (November 9), the Velvet Revolution in Czechoslovakia (November), the violent overthrow of the Ceausescu regime in Romania (December).

The **Berlin Wall** had been built in August 1961 to stop East Germans from escaping to West Germany via West Berlin (about 2.6 million people had left between 1949 and 1961). It came down on **November 9, 1989** when East German Politburo spokesman Gunter Schabowski misread his cue cards at a press conference and announced new travel rules taking effect "immediately." East Berliners walked to the checkpoints. The guards, with no orders to fire and growing crowds in front of them, opened the gates.

**October 3, 1990:** East and West Germany were formally reunified under the West German constitution.

In the Soviet Union itself, things moved fast. In **August 1991**, hardliners in the Politburo, KGB, and military attempted a coup against Gorbachev while he was vacationing in Crimea. Boris Yeltsin, then president of the Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic, climbed onto a tank outside the Russian White House and called for resistance. The coup collapsed within three days. But the post-coup political dynamics turned decisively against the central Soviet government.

In December 1991, the leaders of Russia, Ukraine, and Belarus signed the Belovezha Accords dissolving the Soviet Union and creating the **Commonwealth of Independent States (CIS)**. **December 25, 1991:** Gorbachev resigned. The Soviet flag was lowered over the Kremlin. The USSR formally ceased to exist on December 26, 1991.

<BadFact source="Reagan mythology">
  Reagan's "Tear down this wall!" speech was NOT well-received initially. Many of his own advisors urged him to cut the line; Western media barely covered the speech at the time. It became iconic only after the Wall actually fell two years later, retroactively framing Reagan as having predicted (or even caused) the collapse.
</BadFact>

<Callout type="insight" title="Class poll: did Gorbachev mean to end communism, or did his reforms slip out of control?">
  Pick the position you find most defensible and write a sentence explaining why.

  - **He intended it.** He intended to dismantle Soviet communism in something close to the form it ended up taking; the chaos was the price of doing it.
  - **He intended reform, not end.** He intended to reform communism into a humane democratic socialism, not to end it. The collapse was a failure from his perspective.
  - **He lost control.** He started reforms with limited intentions and lost control as forces he had not anticipated overtook events.
  - **Structurally doomed.** Whatever Gorbachev intended, the Soviet system was already structurally doomed by the late 1980s; his reforms only changed the timing.
</Callout>

<RoughNotation type="underline" color="#dc2626">The Cold War ended without a hot war between the superpowers.</RoughNotation> That is the structural fact that has to be explained. The competing explanations: nuclear deterrence stabilized the standoff by raising the cost of war; containment exhausted the USSR economically; Gorbachev was a contingent individual whose specific decisions made the peaceful exit possible; the Soviet system was not legitimate enough to defend itself. Most historians take some combination of all four.

{/* === END OF ERA 12 === */}

## Putting it all together

Five centuries down. Twelve eras. About 100 key terms. Sixty marquee dates. Six wars on the tier of "if Europe loses, the world is different." We close with the patterns that recur, because the patterns are what the test is actually testing.

<Callout type="tip" title="What this section is for">
  Cause-effect reasoning across eras is what separates a memorizing student from a thinking one. The mock test will reward students who can connect the French Revolution's pattern to the Russian Revolution's, or the Munich pattern to the Yalta pattern, or the print-Reformation pattern to the Internet age. Read this section as a cross-era index of recurring shapes.
</Callout>

### Five recurring patterns

**1. Revolution then reaction.** Revolutions rarely settle into the order their authors imagined. The French Revolution gave us the Reign of Terror, then Napoleon, then the Bourbons restored. The Russian Revolution gave us Stalin. 1989 in Eastern Europe gave us, eventually, illiberal populism in some places. The pattern: a revolution displaces a settled order, the displaced order's constituencies do not disappear, and the new order accommodates the predecessor's ghosts.

**2. War, settlement, resentment, war.** Vienna 1815 worked for a generation, then cracked under nationalism. Versailles 1919 humiliated Germany without crippling it; twenty years later Hitler reversed every clause. Yalta and Potsdam papered over a basic disagreement about Eastern Europe and produced the Cold War. Settlements that humiliate without disabling tend to produce the next war.

**3. Ideology, mass movement, totalitarianism.** The arc to totalitarianism takes three phases: a small number of intellectuals develop the framework (Marx 1848, Mussolini 1919, Hitler 1924), a crisis makes it attractive at scale (WWI, the Depression), the mass movement captures the state, often legally (Lenin 1917, Mussolini 1922, Hitler 1933), and dismantles the institutions that constrained it.

**4. Technology shifts power.** Printing press into Reformation. Atlantic shipping into overseas empire. Steam and rails into industrial Europe and conscript armies. Atomic weapons into Cold War deterrence. Computing into Soviet collapse. The political form an era takes is constrained by what its dominant technologies make possible.

**5. Empire then decolonization.** First empires 1500s to 1700s. New Imperialism 1870 to 1914 partitioned Africa and Asia. Mandates 1919 reorganized the substance under new legal cover. WWII bankrupted the empires; the wave of independence 1945 to 1965 created most of today's nation-state map. Economic and cultural influence outlast formal empire.

{/* === END OF PUTTING IT ALL TOGETHER === */}

## Final exam mock test

A 50-minute mock final under exam conditions. Work through the 25-question main bank, then click "New quiz" to cycle through six alternate banks (5 questions each, 55 total).

    { q: "Which 1942 to 1943 battle on the Volga was the European turning point of WWII?", choices: ["Kursk", "Stalingrad", "Berlin", "Leningrad"], answer: 1, explain: "Soviets encircled and destroyed the German Sixth Army." },

### Vocabulary drill: 15 critical key terms

<TypingQuiz
  title="Vocab drill"
  promptLabel="Type the term that matches"
  acceptArticles="ignore"
  pairs={[
    { en: "A ruler with unlimited power who controls all of society. Example: Louis XIV.", es: "Absolute monarch", explain: "Three pillars: army, bureaucracy, Church." },
    { en: "Copernicus's 1543 model placing the Sun at the center.", es: "Heliocentric Theory", explain: "Confirmed by Galileo's telescope." },
    { en: "June 1789 oath where the Third Estate vowed not to disband until France had a constitution.", es: "Tennis Court Oath", explain: "First formal break from the Old Regime." },
    { en: "1793 to 1794. Robespierre's executions of political opponents at industrial scale.", es: "Reign of Terror", explain: "About 17,000 official executions." },
    { en: "1804 unified French civil code that became Napoleon's most durable legacy.", es: "Napoleonic Code", explain: "Equality before the law and protection of private property." },
    { en: "1814 to 1815 conference that redrew Europe.", es: "Congress of Vienna", explain: "Metternich, Castlereagh, Talleyrand." },
    { en: "Doctrine that government should not interfere in the economy.", es: "Laissez-faire", explain: "Adam Smith and 19th-century classical liberalism." },
    { en: "1884 to 1885 conference where European powers set rules for partitioning Africa.", es: "Berlin Conference", explain: "No African delegates present." },
    { en: "German plan to defeat France in six weeks.", es: "Schlieffen Plan", explain: "Stopped at the Marne in September 1914." },
    { en: "War guilt clause of the Treaty of Versailles.", es: "Article 231", explain: "Provided the legal basis for reparations." },
    { en: "Stalin's centralized economic blueprints, beginning 1928.", es: "Five-Year Plans", explain: "Massive industrial output at massive human cost." },
    { en: "The German government from 1919 to 1933.", es: "Weimar Republic", explain: "Crippled by Versailles, hyperinflation, and Article 48." },
    { en: "Granting concessions to an aggressive power to avoid war.", es: "Appeasement", explain: "Chamberlain at Munich 1938." },
    { en: "1948 to 1952 program transferring about 12.5 billion dollars in U.S. aid to rebuild Western Europe.", es: "Marshall Plan", explain: "Stalin forbade Eastern European participation." },
    { en: "Russian for 'openness.' Gorbachev's mid-1980s policy of permitting public criticism.", es: "Glasnost", explain: "Loosened censorship and surfaced grievances." }
  ]}

## Mnemonics

<Callout type="tip" title="MAIN: causes of World War I">
**M**ilitarism, **A**lliances, **I**mperialism, **N**ationalism.
</Callout>

<Callout type="tip" title="ABC: three pillars of absolutism">
**A**rmy, **B**ureaucracy, **C**hurch.
</Callout>

<Callout type="tip" title="CCNAW: the five questions for any era">
**C**ause, **C**ourse, **N**otable figures, **A**ftermath, **W**hy it matters.
</Callout>

<Callout type="tip" title="CALMS: Industrial Revolution preconditions">
**C**oal and iron, **A**gricultural revolution, **L**aissez-faire, **M**arkets, **S**urplus capital.
</Callout>

<Callout type="tip" title="WPLDS: WWI to WWII">
**W**eimar instability, **P**unitive Versailles, **L**ack of U.S. engagement, **D**epression, **S**oviets sign with Hitler 1939.
</Callout>

<Callout type="tip" title="PCS: cause-effect-significance">
**P**recipitating cause, **C**onsequences, **S**ignificance.
</Callout>

## Common pitfalls

<Callout type="warning" title="Marshall Plan dollar amount">
About 12.5 billion dollars between 1948 and 1952, not 13.
</Callout>

<Callout type="warning" title="Russian Revolution dates">
The two 1917 revolutions are commonly called February and October from the old Julian calendar. By the modern Gregorian calendar they took place in March and November.
</Callout>

<Callout type="warning" title="Appeasement was not just cowardice">
Chamberlain understood it as buying time to rearm Britain. The exam-correct critique is that it failed because Hitler interpreted concessions as weakness.
</Callout>

<Callout type="warning" title="Mussolini was Fascist, not Nazi">
Italian Fascism (1922) and German Nazism (1933) overlap on authoritarianism but differ on systematic racial antisemitism.
</Callout>

<Callout type="warning" title="Reign of Terror demographics">
About 85% of the roughly 17,000 official guillotinings were peasants and middle class, not nobles.
</Callout>

<Callout type="warning" title="Hitler was appointed, not coup'd">
Hitler became Chancellor on January 30, 1933 by legal appointment from Hindenburg.
</Callout>

<Callout type="warning" title="Versailles did not 'cause' WWII alone">
A major contributor among many: Great Depression, Weimar fragility, League failure, Munich, Nazi ideology, Nazi-Soviet Pact.
</Callout>

<Callout type="warning" title="Three distinct Cold-War endpoints">
Wall fell November 9, 1989. Cold War generally dated 1989 to 1991. USSR dissolved December 25, 1991.
</Callout>

## Cheat sheet

| Era | Years | Key cause | Key effect | Significance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Absolutism | 1600s | Religious wars and noble revolts | Versailles, standing armies, mercantilism | Sets the frame the Enlightenment will challenge |
| Sci Rev + Enlightenment | 1543 to 1789 | Telescope, printing press, religious pluralism | Scientific method; natural rights; separation of powers | Reason replaces tradition |
| French Revolution | 1789 to 1799 | Bankruptcy, bad harvests, inflexible Old Regime | Tennis Court Oath, Bastille, Rights of Man, Reign of Terror | First modern political revolution |
| Napoleon | 1799 to 1815 | Power vacuum after Directory | Code, Continental System, Russia, Waterloo | Spreads revolutionary law while restoring autocracy |
| Vienna and nationalism | 1815 to 1871 | Conservative shock at the Revolution | Balance of power, Concert of Europe, unifications | Sets up the alliance grid that fails in 1914 |
| Industrial Revolution | 1750 to 1900 | Coal, iron, freed labor, accumulated capital | Factory system, urbanization, capitalism, socialism | Economic transformation everything political downstream sits on |
| Imperialism | 1870 to 1914 | Industrial demand for raw materials and markets | Berlin Conference rules; partition of Africa | Globalizes European competition |
| World War I | 1914 to 1918 | MAIN causes plus Sarajevo | Trench warfare, Versailles, four empires fall | Ends the long 19th century |
| Russian Revolution | 1917 to 1939 | Tsarist collapse; Lenin's organization | Soviet state, NEP, Five-Year Plans, Great Purge | First communist great power |
| Interwar | 1919 to 1939 | Versailles resentment, Depression, Weimar fragility | Rise of Mussolini and Hitler; Munich | How unstable democracies fail |
| World War II | 1939 to 1945 | Lebensraum, failed appeasement, Nazi-Soviet Pact | Holocaust, Stalingrad, D-Day, atomic bomb | Most destructive event in human history |
| Cold War | 1945 to 1991 | Ideological clash; nuclear weapons | Containment, NATO, Berlin Wall, Cuban Missile Crisis | Forty-six years of bipolar standoff |

## Memorize the dates

Twelve dates that the AP, EOC, and most college European-history finals will assume you can produce on demand. Switch modes (Read, Cloze, First letter, Cold recall) to drill them.

<Memorize
  title="Twelve dates you must know cold"
  attribution="The chronological backbone of modern Europe"
  text={`1648  Peace of Westphalia ends the Thirty Years' War and codifies state sovereignty.
1688  Glorious Revolution: Parliament invites William and Mary; constitutional monarchy.
1789  Storming of the Bastille on July 14 begins the French Revolution.
1815  Battle of Waterloo on June 18; Congress of Vienna redraws the map.
1848  Year of Revolutions sweeps France, the German states, the Habsburgs, Italy.
1871  Bismarck proclaims the German Empire in the Hall of Mirrors at Versailles.
1914  Franz Ferdinand assassinated June 28 in Sarajevo; WWI begins August.
1917  Bolshevik October Revolution; Lenin and Trotsky take power in Petrograd.
1919  Treaty of Versailles signed June 28 in the Hall of Mirrors.
1939  Germany invades Poland September 1; Britain and France declare war September 3.
1945  V-E Day May 8; atomic bombs August 6 and 9; V-J Day August 15.
1989  Berlin Wall falls November 9; the Cold War effectively ends.`}
/>

    { answer: "BOURGEOISIE",    clue: "Marx's name for the industrial middle class that owns the means of production." },

    { answer: "GLASNOST",       clue: "Gorbachev's 'openness' policy of the mid-1980s." },
    { answer: "PERESTROIKA",    clue: "Gorbachev's 'restructuring' of the Soviet economy." },
    { answer: "GUILLOTINE",     clue: "Tool of the Reign of Terror; took Louis XVI's head January 1793." },
    { answer: "JACOBINS",       clue: "Robespierre's radical faction in the French Revolution." },
    { answer: "REPARATIONS",    clue: "Payments imposed on Germany by the 1919 treaty." },
    { answer: "FASCISM",        clue: "Mussolini's ideology; nationalist, militarist, anti-liberal." },
    { answer: "DETENTE",        clue: "1970s easing of U.S.-Soviet tensions; SALT I and Helsinki." },
    { answer: "STALINGRAD",     clue: "Battle on the Volga, 1942 to 1943; European turning point of WWII." }
  ]}

## Synthesis: classify any revolution

A short branching key. Walk it once for each revolution this guide covers and see how the same questions sort them into very different bins. The exam likes to hand you an unfamiliar revolt and ask which type it is; this is the trellis to use.

<DichotomousKey
  title="What kind of revolution is this?"
  yesLabel="Yes"
  noLabel="No"
  tree={{
    question: "Did the revolution overthrow the monarchy?",
    yes: {
      question: "Was its ideological framework Marxist or proto-Marxist?",
      yes: {
        question: "Did the revolution succeed in installing a socialist or communist state that survived more than five years?",
        yes:  { result: "Communist revolution", note: "Russian Revolution 1917, Chinese Revolution 1949, Cuban Revolution 1959. The Bolshevik / vanguard-party model usually applies." },
        no:   { result: "Failed leftist uprising", note: "Spartacist Revolt 1919 (Berlin), Bavarian Soviet Republic 1919, Hungarian Soviet Republic 1919. Crushed within a year, but they leave the political vocabulary changed." }
      },
      no: {
        question: "Did the revolution institutionalize representative or constitutional government?",
        yes:  { result: "Liberal-constitutional revolution", note: "French Revolution 1789 (in its 1789 to 1791 phase), 1848 revolutions where they succeeded, eventually the Russian February Revolution of March 1917." },
        no:   { result: "Charismatic-authoritarian revolution", note: "French Revolution after Thermidor / Napoleon's coup; Mussolini's March on Rome 1922; Hitler's Machtergreifung 1933 (constitutional in form, revolutionary in substance)." }
      }
    },
    no: {
      question: "Did it transfer formal power between elites without mobilizing the broad public?",
      yes: {
        question: "Was the change peaceful?",
        yes:  { result: "Palace revolution / coup", note: "Glorious Revolution 1688 (substantially). Most 18th-century European 'revolutions' fit here." },
        no:   { result: "Coup d'etat", note: "Napoleon's 18 Brumaire 1799; many Latin American and 20th-century examples." }
      },
      no: {
        question: "Was the goal national liberation from external rule?",
        yes:  { result: "National-liberation revolution", note: "Italian unification 1861, German unification 1871, Polish independence 1918, Indian independence 1947, the wave of African decolonizations 1956 to 1975." },
        no:   { result: "Social or cultural revolution", note: "Industrial Revolution (technological-economic), Scientific Revolution (epistemic), Sexual Revolution 1960s. They reshape society without changing political sovereignty." }
      }
    }
  }}
/>
